The sign is tied to the metric's $-2Hdu^2$ convention.

#### Homogeneous wavefront of a plane gravitational wave

↑ **Parent:** [Plane-fronted gravitational wave](#plane-fronted-gravitational-wave)

For a Brinkmann plane wave whose transverse profile is quadratic, Killing fields obtained from solutions of transverse oscillator equations act transitively on every constant-retarded-time wavefront. Each wavefront is therefore a homogeneous null hypersurface.

#### Rosen coordinates for a plane gravitational wave

↑ **Parent:** [Plane-fronted gravitational wave](#plane-fronted-gravitational-wave)

Rosen coordinates put a plane wave into the transverse-metric form

$$
ds^2=-du\,dv+g_{AB}(u)dx^A dx^B.
$$

For diagonal $g_{AB}=\operatorname{diag}(f^2,g^2)$, the only potentially nonzero [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor) component is $R_{uu}=-f''/f-g''/g$.

##### Curvature of a diagonal plane wave in Rosen coordinates

↑ **Parent:** [Rosen coordinates for a plane gravitational wave](#rosen-coordinates-for-a-plane-gravitational-wave)

For $g=-dt^2+A(u)^2dx^2+B(u)^2dy^2+dz^2$, $u=t-z$, with nowhere-zero smooth $A,B$, take $e^0=dt$, $e^1=A\,dx$, $e^2=B\,dy$, $e^3=dz$, $\ell=du$. In signature $(-,+,+,+)$, [Cartan's first structure equation](connection-1-form.md#cartan-s-first-structure-equation) and [metric compatibility](fiber-bundle.md#metric-compatibility) give the lower-index [connection 1-forms](connection-1-form.md) $\omega_{01}=\omega_{13}=-(A'/A)e^1$ and $\omega_{02}=\omega_{23}=-(B'/B)e^2$. [Cartan's second structure equation](connection-1-form.md#cartan-s-second-structure-equation) gives $\Theta_{01}=\Theta_{13}=-(A''/A)\ell\wedge e^1$ and $\Theta_{02}=\Theta_{23}=-(B''/B)\ell\wedge e^2$. All other independent [curvature 2-forms](connection-1-form.md#curvature-2-form) vanish: in particular the $12$ component cancels between the time and longitudinal terms. Contracting $R^a{}_{bad}$ yields $\operatorname{Ric}=-(A''/A+B''/B)du\otimes du$. Thus the [Vacuum Einstein equations](#vacuum-einstein-equations) are $A''/A+B''/B=0$, while nonzero $A''/A=-B''/B$ gives opposite transverse tidal curvatures and [plus polarization](#plus-polarization) relative to these axes.

#### Gravitational-wave pulse

↑ **Parent:** [Plane-fronted gravitational wave](#plane-fronted-gravitational-wave)

A gravitational-wave pulse has curvature supported in a bounded interval of [retarded time](electromagnetism.md#retarded-time). Spacetime can be flat both before and after the pulse even though freely falling bodies retain a displacement or relative velocity produced while the pulse passed.

##### Gravitational-wave memory effect

↑ **Parent:** [Gravitational-wave pulse](#gravitational-wave-pulse)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational-wave_memory_effect)

The gravitational-wave memory effect is a lasting change in the relative configuration of freely falling test bodies after a transient [gravitational wave](#gravitational-wave) has passed.

###### Displacement memory

↑ **Parent:** [Gravitational-wave memory effect](#gravitational-wave-memory-effect)

Displacement memory is a persistent change in the separation of freely falling bodies after a [gravitational-wave pulse](#gravitational-wave-pulse).

###### Velocity memory

↑ **Parent:** [Gravitational-wave memory effect](#gravitational-wave-memory-effect)

Velocity memory is a persistent change in the relative velocity of freely falling bodies after a [gravitational-wave pulse](#gravitational-wave-pulse).

## Covariant derivative

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covariant_derivative)

A connection defines covariant differentiation by adding one connection term for each contravariant index and subtracting one for each covariant index.

### Covariant divergence formula

↑ **Parent:** [Covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative)

The [Christoffel trace identity](riemannian-geometry.md#christoffel-trace-identity) converts the [covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative) of a [vector field](calculus.md#vector-field) into a coordinate divergence weighted by the metric volume density. This is valid for the [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection). Applied to the raised gradient of a [scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field), it gives $\Box\phi=|g|^{-1/2}\partial_\mu(\sqrt{|g|}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_\nu\phi)$ in Lorentzian signature, the curved [d'Alembert operator](wave-equation.md#d-alembert-operator).

### Covariant divergence

↑ **Parent:** [Covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative)

The covariant divergence contracts a covariant-derivative index with a contravariant tensor index. For a mixed rank-two tensor it is $\nabla_\mu T^\mu{}_{\nu}$.

### Tensor type

↑ **Parent:** [Covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative)

A tensor of type $(r,s)$ has $r$ contravariant and $s$ covariant indices. Taking a covariant derivative adds one covariant index, while an outer tensor product adds the corresponding contravariant and covariant index counts.

#### Tensor component transformation law

↑ **Parent:** [Tensor type](#tensor-type)

Under a coordinate change $x\mapsto x'$, the components of a type-$(r,s)$ tensor acquire one forward Jacobian for each contravariant index and one inverse Jacobian for each covariant index. For a type-$(1,1)$ tensor,

$$
T^{\mu'}{}_{\nu'}
=\frac{\partial x^{\mu'}}{\partial x^\alpha}
\frac{\partial x^\beta}{\partial x^{\nu'}}
T^\alpha{}_{\beta}.
$$

##### Tensor density

↑ **Parent:** [Tensor component transformation law](#tensor-component-transformation-law)

A tensor density of weight $w$ transforms like a [tensor](linear-algebra.md#tensor) with an additional factor $|\det(\partial x/\partial x')|^w$ under $x\mapsto x'$. A scalar density of weight one can be integrated against the coordinate volume element to give an invariant integral. On a spatial hypersurface, $\sqrt h$ is such a scalar density; hence $\pi^{ij}=\sqrt h p^{ij}$ is a tensor density when $p^{ij}$ is an ordinary tensor. The [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection) extends to densities and satisfies $D_i\sqrt h=0$.

#### Identity tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Tensor type](#tensor-type)

The identity tensor is the canonical type-$(1,1)$ tensor that maps each tangent vector to itself. It pairs a covector $\eta$ and vector $V$ as $\eta(V)$, and its components are the [Kronecker delta](linear-algebra.md#kronecker-delta) $\delta^\mu{}_{\nu}$ in every coordinate system.

### Covariant derivative of a covariant tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative)

For $W_{\mu\nu}$,

$$
\nabla_\alpha W_{\mu\nu}
=\partial_\alpha W_{\mu\nu}
-\Gamma^\rho_{\alpha\mu}W_{\rho\nu}
-\Gamma^\rho_{\alpha\nu}W_{\mu\rho}.
$$

### Levi-Civita connection

↑ **Parent:** [Covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levi-Civita_connection)

The Levi-Civita connection is the unique torsion-free connection compatible with the metric; its coefficients are the Christoffel symbols formed from first metric derivatives.

#### Variation of Levi-Civita connection

↑ **Parent:** [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection)

For a family of [Levi-Civita connections](#levi-civita-connection), the [difference of affine connections is a tensor](fiber-bundle.md#difference-of-affine-connections-is-a-tensor). Dividing by the family parameter and taking the [limit](calculus.md#limit-of-a-function) shows that its [variation](calculus-of-variations.md#variation) is [tensorial](fiber-bundle.md#tensoriality). Vary the [metric compatibility](fiber-bundle.md#metric-compatibility) equation and retain the symmetry of the two lower indices. Adding the two cyclic identities and subtracting the third gives the displayed result. In a normal [coordinate frame](differential-geometry.md#coordinate-basis) it is the direct [derivative](calculus.md#derivative) of the Christoffel formula; its [tensorial](fiber-bundle.md#tensoriality) form then holds in every frame.

#### Parallel metrics with a common Levi-Civita connection

↑ **Parent:** [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection)

Two [Riemannian metrics](differential-geometry.md#riemannian-metric) have the same [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection) precisely when their relative positive [self-adjoint](linear-operator-theory.md#self-adjoint-operator) [endomorphism](algebra.md#endomorphism) $A$ is parallel for that connection. [Parallel transport](fiber-bundle.md#parallel-transport) preserves both metrics. Consequently agreement at one point implies agreement everywhere on a [connected](geometry-and-topology.md#connected-space) manifold. Without that agreement, constant scalar multiples always give examples; constant nonproportional Euclidean forms show that a common connection does not force scalar proportionality.

#### Fundamental theorem of Riemannian geometry

↑ **Parent:** [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection)

A [Riemannian metric](differential-geometry.md#riemannian-metric) determines a unique [torsion-free connection](fiber-bundle.md#torsion-free-connection) that is a [metric connection](fiber-bundle.md#metric-connection). Metric compatibility and the torsion identity imply the [Koszul formula](fiber-bundle.md#koszul-formula), which proves uniqueness. Conversely the right-hand side of the [Koszul formula](fiber-bundle.md#koszul-formula) is a smooth one-form in its last vector-field argument. The [musical isomorphism](differential-geometry.md#musical-isomorphism) defines $D_XY$ from it; expanding the [Lie bracket of vector fields](differential-geometry.md#lie-bracket-of-vector-fields) verifies the connection rules, metric compatibility and zero torsion. Thus it constructs the [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection) intrinsically.

#### Lie derivative of an affine connection

↑ **Parent:** [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection)

The Lie derivative of a connection is the tensor

$$
(\mathcal L_X\nabla)_YZ
=\mathcal L_X(\nabla_YZ)-\nabla_{\mathcal L_XY}Z-\nabla_Y(\mathcal L_XZ).
$$

An isometry preserves the Levi-Civita connection, so $\mathcal L_K\nabla=0$ for a [Killing vector field](#killing-vector-field).

## Riemann curvature tensor

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riemann_curvature_tensor)

The Riemann tensor measures the failure of covariant derivatives to commute.

### Curvature sign convention

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

Definitions of the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) can differ by an overall sign, independent of the [metric signature](topology.md#metric-signature). Reversing that definition reverses the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor), [Ricci scalar](#ricci-scalar) and [Einstein tensor](#einstein-tensor), as well as the [curvature](differential-geometry.md#curvature) side of the [geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation) identity and the [Palatini identity](#palatini-identity). A derivation must choose the contraction and [derivative](calculus.md#derivative) ordering explicitly before combining formulas from different conventions. Changing a [metric signature](topology.md#metric-signature) alone is a separate operation.

### Double divergence of a contravariant tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection), a contravariant rank-two [tensor](linear-algebra.md#tensor) satisfies $T^{ab}{}_{;ab}=T^{ab}{}_{;ba}$ without itself being symmetric. The [Ricci identity](#curvature-commutator-on-a-covariant-tensor) gives their difference as $R_{pb}(T^{pb}-T^{bp})$, which vanishes because the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor) is symmetric. Zero torsion alone for a general affine connection does not ensure this symmetry.

### Pair symmetry from the normal-coordinate curvature formula

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

With convention $R(X,Y)=\nabla_{[X,Y]}-[\nabla_X,\nabla_Y]$, and $R_{ij,kl}=g(R(\partial_i,\partial_j)\partial_k,\partial_l)$, normal coordinates give

$$
R_{ij,kl}=\tfrac12(g_{jk,il}+g_{il,jk}-g_{jl,ik}-g_{ik,jl})
$$

at their center. Symmetry of the metric and commutation of second derivatives prove pair exchange. The opposite curvature convention changes the formula's overall sign but preserves all these algebraic symmetries.

### Bianchi identities from covariant derivative commutators

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

The [first Bianchi identity](#first-bianchi-identity) follows by applying cyclic curvature commutators to the gradient of an arbitrary [scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field): its symmetric Hessian makes the third-derivative terms cancel, leaving $R_{abc}{}^d+R_{bca}{}^d+R_{cab}{}^d=0$. For the [second Bianchi identity](#second-bianchi-identity), write the vector-bundle derivative as $D_a=\partial_a+\Gamma_a$, so $[D_b,D_c]=\mathcal R_{bc}$. The operator [Jacobi identity](lie-algebra.md#jacobi-identity) gives the cyclic sum of $\partial_a\mathcal R_{bc}+[\Gamma_a,\mathcal R_{bc}]$ equal to zero. Restoring the connection terms on the two curvature-form indices gives precisely $\nabla_aR_{bcd}{}^e+\nabla_bR_{cad}{}^e+\nabla_cR_{abd}{}^e=0$: the additional terms cancel because the connection is torsion-free and curvature is antisymmetric in its first pair.

### Curvature commutator is a tensor derivation

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a torsion-free [linear connection on a manifold](fiber-bundle.md#affine-connection), the commutator of two [covariant derivatives](#covariant-derivative) obeys the tensor-product [Leibniz rule](calculus.md#leibniz-rule): mixed first-derivative terms cancel after antisymmetrizing. It vanishes on [scalar fields](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field), so $\Delta_{ab}(fQ)=f\Delta_{ab}Q$; its action is therefore pointwise tensorial. Its vector action defines the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor). Applying the commutator to a scalar contraction proves that every covariant tensor index contributes minus the corresponding curvature action, while every contravariant index contributes plus. Torsion-free does not by itself imply [metric compatibility](fiber-bundle.md#metric-compatibility).

### Three-dimensional curvature from the Ricci tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

In dimension three the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) is $R_{ijpq}=g_{ip}S_{jq}-g_{iq}S_{jp}-g_{jp}S_{iq}+g_{jq}S_{ip}$, where $S$ is the [Schouten tensor](#schouten-tensor). Consequently every three-dimensional [Ricci-flat Riemannian manifold](second-fundamental-form.md#ricci-flat-riemannian-manifold) is a [flat Riemannian manifold](second-fundamental-form.md#flat-manifold). The implication fails in higher dimensions.

### Kretschmann scalar

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

The Kretschmann scalar is the scalar [tensor contraction](linear-algebra.md#tensor-contraction) of two copies of the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor). In [Schwarzschild spacetime](#schwarzschild-spacetime), with metric coefficient $1-2M/r$ and $G=c=1$, it is $48M^2/r^6$. Divergence of this scalar along a curve approaching a proposed regular point proves that there is no twice continuously differentiable, nondegenerate metric extension through that point: such an extension would have continuous finite curvature components and hence a finite scalar contraction. This gives a coordinate-independent test of a [curvature singularity](#curvature-singularity). Boundedness of this single scalar does not prove regularity.

### Curvature commutator on a covariant tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a rank-two covariant tensor, the derivative commutator contributes one curvature action on each index:

$$
[\nabla_\alpha,\nabla_\beta]W_{\mu\nu}
=-R^\rho{}_{\mu\alpha\beta}W_{\rho\nu}
-R^\rho{}_{\nu\alpha\beta}W_{\mu\rho}.
$$

### Curvature commutator on a contravariant tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a type-$(2,0)$ tensor, the [Ricci identity](#curvature-commutator-on-a-covariant-tensor) acts once on each contravariant index:

$$
[\nabla_\alpha,\nabla_\beta]T^{\mu\nu}
=R^\mu{}_{\rho\alpha\beta}T^{\rho\nu}
+R^\nu{}_{\rho\alpha\beta}T^{\mu\rho}.
$$

### Geodesic deviation

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geodesic_deviation)

For a family of affinely parametrized geodesics with tangent $U$ and connecting field $J$, the Jacobi equation in the convention used here is

$$
\nabla_U\nabla_UJ=R(U,J)U.
$$

It describes the relative acceleration produced by spacetime curvature.

#### Local tidal acceleration scaling

↑ **Parent:** [Geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation)

The [geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation) equation makes the relative gravitational acceleration proportional to the separation $L$ and a typical [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) component $\mathcal R$. Thus tidal acceleration vanishes as $L\to0$ at fixed bounded curvature. A uniform [electric field](electromagnetism.md#electric-field) can give different accelerations $qE/m$ at the same point, independently of separation. Relative tidal changes accumulated over a fixed long time need not vanish as a fraction of the initial separation; a local spacetime experiment must control its duration as well as its spatial size.

#### Conserved deviation energy in constant curvature

↑ **Parent:** [Geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation)

In [constant sectional curvature](#constant-sectional-curvature) $\kappa$, a [deviation vector](riemannian-geometry.md#deviation-vector) orthogonal to a unit [timelike geodesic](#timelike-geodesic) obeys $D^2S=\kappa S$, where $D=\nabla_T$. Therefore $K=g(DS,DS)-\kappa g(S,S)$ is constant, since its derivative is $2g(DS,D^2S)-2\kappa g(S,DS)=0$. Its squared length $f=g(S,S)$ satisfies $\ddot f=2K+4\kappa f$. In four spacetime dimensions, $\kappa=R/12$.

##### Exponential and oscillatory geodesic deviation

↑ **Parent:** [Conserved deviation energy in constant curvature](#conserved-deviation-energy-in-constant-curvature)

In a [parallel-propagated orthonormal frame](#parallel-propagated-orthonormal-frame), orthogonal [geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation) in [constant sectional curvature](#constant-sectional-curvature) reduces to three scalar equations $\ddot S^i=\kappa S^i$. For $\kappa>0$, $S=Ae^{\sqrt\kappa\tau}+Be^{-\sqrt\kappa\tau}$: generic initial data grow exponentially, but the pure decaying mode is a counterexample to universal growth. Initially comoving neighbors have $S=S(0)\cosh(\sqrt\kappa\tau)$. For $\kappa<0$, $S=A\cos(\sqrt{-\kappa}\tau)+B\sin(\sqrt{-\kappa}\tau)$, so the deviation is bounded and oscillatory. Simultaneous focusing requires compatible vector initial data and is not automatic for every oscillatory solution.

#### Orthogonality of proper-time deviation vectors

↑ **Parent:** [Geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation)

For a [geodesic variation](riemannian-geometry.md#geodesic-variation) of unit [timelike geodesics](#timelike-geodesic), [proper time](special-relativity.md#proper-time) normalization holds across the entire variation: $g(T,T)=-1$. [Metric compatibility](fiber-bundle.md#metric-compatibility) and $\nabla_TS=\nabla_ST$ then give $d g(S,T)/d\tau=\tfrac12S(g(T,T))=0$. Consequently an initially orthogonal [deviation vector](riemannian-geometry.md#deviation-vector) stays orthogonal. The common normalization is essential: an arbitrary family of affinely parametrized curves with different tangent norms does not imply this conclusion.

#### Jacobi field

↑ **Parent:** [Geodesic deviation](#geodesic-deviation)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobi_field)

A Jacobi field is the infinitesimal connecting vector between neighboring members of a one-parameter family of geodesics. It satisfies the geodesic-deviation equation along the reference geodesic.

##### Jacobi fields vanishing at their initial point

↑ **Parent:** [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field)

For $\gamma(t)=\exp_p(tV)$, every [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field) with $J(0)=0$ is uniquely of the form

$$
J(t)=(d\exp_p)_{tV}(tB),\qquad B=D_tJ(0)\in T_pM.
$$

Differentiate the [geodesic variation](riemannian-geometry.md#geodesic-variation) $\exp_p(t(V+sB))$ in $s$ to obtain the formula. Uniqueness of the Jacobi equation with prescribed initial value and derivative shows that all such fields are obtained. A nontrivial field vanishing at $t=L>0$ therefore corresponds to a nonzero vector in the kernel of $(d\exp_p)_{LV}$, giving the [conjugate point](calculus-of-variations.md#conjugate-point) criterion.

##### Jacobi curvature operator

↑ **Parent:** [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field)

For the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) convention $R(X,Y)Z=\nabla_X\nabla_YZ-\nabla_Y\nabla_XZ-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z$, define $\mathcal R_X(v)=R(v,X)X$. Curvature pair interchange makes this operator self-adjoint. If $v\perp X$, then $\langle\mathcal R_Xv,v\rangle=K(v,X)|v|^2|X|^2$. The [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field) equation is $D_t^2J+\mathcal R_{\dot\gamma}J=0$. The operator $v\mapsto R(X,v)X$ is $-\mathcal R_X$ and is likewise self-adjoint; this sign distinction matters when testing curvature inequalities.

##### Jacobi fields from conjugation at a central endpoint

↑ **Parent:** [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field)

For a [Lie group](lie-theory.md#lie-group) with a [bi-invariant Riemannian metric](lie-theory.md#bi-invariant-riemannian-metric), vary $\gamma(t)=e^{tA}$ by conjugation: $F(s,t)=e^{sX}e^{tA}e^{-sX}$. Its [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field) is $J_X(t)=Xe^{tA}-e^{tA}X$. If $e^A$ is central, all these fields vanish at both endpoints. Their space has dimension $\dim\mathfrak g-\dim\mathfrak z(A)$, since the kernel is the [centralizer of an element of a Lie algebra](lie-algebra.md#centralizer-of-an-element-of-a-lie-algebra).

##### Endpoint-vanishing normal Jacobi fields have dimension at most n-1

↑ **Parent:** [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field)

In dimension $n\ge2$, along a nonconstant [geodesic](riemannian-geometry.md#geodesic), such a [Jacobi field](#jacobi-field) is determined injectively by $D_tJ(0)$, which lies in the $(n-1)$-dimensional normal space. On the round unit sphere the geodesic from a point to its antipode, parametrized with speed $\pi$ on $[0,1]$, has fields $J(t)=\sin(\pi t)E(t)$ for all parallel normal fields $E$, attaining the bound. For a constant geodesic the endpoint-vanishing Jacobi field space is zero.

### Symmetric Hessian of a scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a torsion-free connection, $U_\mu=\partial_\mu\phi$ satisfies $\nabla_\alpha U_\beta=\nabla_\beta U_\alpha$.

#### Third covariant derivatives of a scalar

↑ **Parent:** [Symmetric Hessian of a scalar field](#symmetric-hessian-of-a-scalar-field)

Use the sequential convention $\varphi_{;lmn}=\nabla_n\nabla_m\nabla_l\varphi$ and define $R$ by $X^a{}_{;mn}-X^a{}_{;nm}=R^a{}_{bmn}X^b$. The [symmetric Hessian of a scalar field](#symmetric-hessian-of-a-scalar-field) gives symmetry in $l,m$, but the [curvature commutator on a covariant tensor](#curvature-commutator-on-a-covariant-tensor) gives $\varphi_{;l[mn]}=-R^p{}_{lmn}\varphi_{;p}/2$. Averaging the three distinct permutations yields $\varphi_{;(lm)n}=\varphi_{;(lmn)}-(R^p{}_{lmn}+R^p{}_{mln})\varphi_{;p}/3$. Thus a scalar's third covariant derivative is not generally totally symmetric.

### First Bianchi identity

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For a torsion-free connection, $R^\mu{}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+R^\mu{}_{\beta\gamma\alpha}+R^\mu{}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}=0$.

### Second Bianchi identity

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

For the [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection), the cyclic covariant derivative of curvature vanishes: $\nabla_{[e}R^a{}_{|b|cd]}=0$. Contracting this identity gives the [contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity).

### Weyl tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weyl_tensor)

The Weyl tensor is the completely trace-free part of the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor). In four dimensions,

$$
\begin{aligned}
C_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
={}&R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
-\frac12(
g_{\alpha\gamma}R_{\beta\delta}
+g_{\beta\delta}R_{\alpha\gamma}\\
&\qquad
-g_{\alpha\delta}R_{\beta\gamma}
-g_{\beta\gamma}R_{\alpha\delta})
+\frac R6(
g_{\alpha\gamma}g_{\beta\delta}
-g_{\alpha\delta}g_{\beta\gamma}).
\end{aligned}
$$

#### Magnetic part of the Weyl tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor)

In four spacetime dimensions, relative to a timelike [unit normal](differential-geometry.md#unit-normal) $n$, define $B_{ab}=\tfrac12\epsilon_{acde}C^{de}{}_{bf}n^cn^f$, with the chosen spacetime [metric volume tensor](differential-form.md#metric-volume-tensor). This is the dual tidal part of the [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor). Normal contractions vanish by curvature and volume-tensor antisymmetry. Its sign depends on the specified orientation and dualization convention.

#### Electric part of the Weyl tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor)

Relative to a timelike [unit normal](differential-geometry.md#unit-normal) $n$, the electric part is $E_{ab}=C_{acbd}n^cn^d$. It describes the tidal component of the [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor) measured by that observer. Antisymmetry in each curvature pair makes it spatial in both slots; pair symmetry and vanishing traces make it symmetric and trace-free.

#### Trace-free property of the Weyl tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor)

Every contraction of the Weyl tensor vanishes. Contracting its first and third indices directly cancels the Ricci and scalar-curvature terms; antisymmetry in each pair and symmetry under exchanging the two pairs reduce all other contractions to this one or make them vanish immediately.

#### Weyl tensor of a conformally flat metric

↑ **Parent:** [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor)

Every [conformally flat metric](#conformally-flat-metric) in dimension at least four has identically zero Weyl tensor. The Weyl tensor is the obstruction to local conformal flatness in dimensions at least four.

### First-pair antisymmetry of the Riemann tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

Applying the curvature commutator to the parallel metric gives $R_{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}=-R_{\alpha\mu\beta\gamma}$.

### Ricci tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

The Ricci tensor is the contraction $R_{\alpha\beta}=R^\mu{}_{\alpha\mu\beta}$ and is symmetric for the Levi-Civita connection.

#### Schouten tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor)

For dimension $n\geq3$, the Schouten tensor packages the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor) and [scalar curvature](second-fundamental-form.md#scalar-curvature) in the trace part of the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor). In dimension three it is $S_{ij}=R_{ij}-Rg_{ij}/4$, and the full [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) is reconstructed from it.

#### Ricci tensor in two dimensions

↑ **Parent:** [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor)

The algebraic symmetries of the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) leave only one independent curvature component in two dimensions and force

$$
R_{\alpha\beta}=\frac12g_{\alpha\beta}R.
$$

Consequently the [Einstein tensor](#einstein-tensor) vanishes identically.

##### Two-dimensional general relativity

↑ **Parent:** [Ricci tensor in two dimensions](#ricci-tensor-in-two-dimensions)

With no cosmological term, the two-dimensional [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) reduce identically on the geometric side to $G_{\alpha\beta}=0$. They impose $T_{\alpha\beta}=0$ and supply no local metric dynamics; the Einstein-Hilbert action is topological.

#### Contracted Bianchi identity

↑ **Parent:** [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor)

The Ricci tensor and scalar satisfy $\nabla^\alpha R_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac12\nabla_\beta R$.

##### Double divergence of the Riemann tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity)

For the [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection), the [second Bianchi identity](#second-bianchi-identity) gives $\nabla_iR^{ik}{}_{mn}=\nabla_mR^k{}_n-\nabla_nR^k{}_m$. Applying another [covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative) and the [contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity) reduces its divergence to a commuting scalar Hessian plus curvature commutators on the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor). The latter are the difference of symmetric tensors: a square of the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor) and $R^{ab}R_{anbm}$, which is symmetric in $m,n$ by curvature pair exchange and Ricci symmetry. Both differences vanish. This identity does not require vacuum field equations and is independent of the overall curvature sign convention.

##### Einstein tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein_tensor)

The Einstein tensor is

$$
G_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac12Rg_{\mu\nu}.
$$

The contracted Bianchi identity and metric compatibility give  
$\nabla^\nu G_{\mu\nu}=0$, ensuring compatibility of the Einstein equations with covariant stress-energy conservation.

###### Flat FLRW Einstein-tensor divergence

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein tensor](#einstein-tensor)

For $ds^2=-dt^2+a(t)^2d\mathbf x^2$, the nonzero connection coefficients are

$$
\Gamma^t_{ij}=a\dot a\,\delta_{ij},
\qquad
\Gamma^i_{tj}=\Gamma^i_{jt}=H\delta^i_j.
$$

With $G_t^t=-3H^2$ and  
$G_i^j=-(2\ddot a/a+H^2)\delta_i^j$, direct substitution into the mixed-tensor covariant derivative gives zero.

###### de Sitter scale factor in flat slicing

↑ **Parent:** [Flat FLRW Einstein-tensor divergence](#flat-flrw-einstein-tensor-divergence)

The vacuum equation with positive cosmological constant gives  
$H^2=\Lambda/3$. Its expanding flat-slicing solution is

$$
a(t)=a_0e^{\sqrt{\Lambda/3}\,t}.
$$

#### Constant sectional curvature

↑ **Parent:** [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constant_sectional_curvature)

A pseudo-Riemannian manifold has constant sectional curvature $K$ when $R_{abcd}=K(g_{ac}g_{bd}-g_{ad}g_{bc})$. In dimension $n$, its Ricci tensor and scalar are $R_{ab}=(n-1)Kg_{ab}$ and $R=n(n-1)K$.

##### Schur theorem in pseudo-Riemannian geometry

↑ **Parent:** [Constant sectional curvature](#constant-sectional-curvature)

In dimension greater than two, if sectional curvature is pointwise independent of the two-plane, the contracted Bianchi identity forces that curvature to be constant on each connected component.

The positive-definite specialization is [Schur's lemma](riemannian-geometry.md#schur-s-lemma-riemannian-geometry).

### Penrose wave equation

↑ **Parent:** [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor)

In a Ricci-flat spacetime, the Penrose wave equation is a nonlinear covariant wave equation for the Riemann tensor. Its principal part is $\nabla^e\nabla_eR_{abcd}$ and its lower-order terms are quadratic contractions of curvature.

## Normal coordinates

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_coordinates)

At any spacetime point, normal coordinates make the metric equal to the Minkowski metric and all Christoffel symbols vanish at that point.

### Fermi coordinates

↑ **Parent:** [Normal coordinates](#normal-coordinates)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermi_coordinates)

[Fermi coordinates](#fermi-coordinates) transport a local frame along a timelike [worldline](special-relativity.md#world-line). Along a [timelike geodesic](#timelike-geodesic), a parallel-transported orthonormal frame defines [Fermi normal coordinates](#fermi-coordinates) in which the [metric tensor](#metric-tensor) is Minkowskian and the [Christoffel symbols](riemannian-geometry.md#christoffel-symbol) vanish on the worldline. Curvature first appears in terms quadratic in the spatial distance from it, so finite laboratories retain [tidal forces](classical-mechanics.md#tidal-force).

### Lorentz frame

↑ **Parent:** [Normal coordinates](#normal-coordinates)

A [Lorentz frame](#lorentz-frame) at a spacetime event is an orthonormal tangent basis with metric $\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,1,1)$. Future-oriented frames are related by proper orthochronous [Lorentz transformations](special-relativity.md#lorentz-transformation). Any such basis can be realized at the event by [normal coordinates](#normal-coordinates).

## Orthonormal frame in spacetime

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)

An orthonormal frame $\{e_a\}$ satisfies $g(e_a,e_b)=\eta_{ab}$. Any two such frames differ by a local Lorentz transformation.

### Parallel-propagated orthonormal frame

↑ **Parent:** [Orthonormal frame in spacetime](#orthonormal-frame-in-spacetime)

Along a curve with tangent $T$, a frame is parallel propagated when $\nabla_Te_a=0$. Metric compatibility makes every inner product $g(e_a,e_b)$ constant along the curve, so an initially orthonormal frame remains orthonormal.

### Orthonormal coframe in spacetime

↑ **Parent:** [Orthonormal frame in spacetime](#orthonormal-frame-in-spacetime)

The dual coframe obeys $\theta^a(e_b)=\delta^a_b$ and reconstructs the metric as $g=\eta_{ab}\theta^a\otimes\theta^b$.

#### Curved spacetime gamma matrices

↑ **Parent:** [Orthonormal coframe in spacetime](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime)

An orthonormal coframe converts fixed flat [gamma matrices](algebra.md#gamma-matrices) to coordinate-index Clifford multiplication. Its metric identity gives the curved anticommutation relation. The [spin connection](connection-1-form.md#spin-connection) is chosen so that these matrices are covariantly constant under the combined spin and tensor connection, which is the tetrad postulate in Clifford form.

#### Zweibein

↑ **Parent:** [Orthonormal coframe in spacetime](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime)

A zweibein is a two-dimensional [orthonormal coframe in spacetime](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime): its components obey $h_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{AB}e^A{}_{\mu}e^B{}_{\nu}$. It converts worldsheet coordinate indices into local Lorentz-frame indices and defines curved gamma matrices $\gamma^\mu=e_A{}^\mu\gamma^A$. The associated [spin connection](connection-1-form.md#spin-connection) supplies the [covariant derivative](#covariant-derivative) of worldsheet spinors. In flat [conformal gauge](string-theory.md#conformal-gauge) the coframe can be constant.

#### Connection 1-form

↑ **Parent:** [Orthonormal coframe in spacetime](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime)

[This section is present in another page, follow this link to view it.](connection-1-form.md)

## Stress-energy tensor

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress-energy_tensor)

The stress-energy tensor describes local energy density, momentum density, and stress. Compatibility with the [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) requires covariant conservation $\nabla^\mu T_{\mu\nu}=0$.

### Hilbert stress-energy tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

The metric variational stress tensor is defined by the matter action's response to a metric change. With signature $(+---)$ and the corresponding usual matter-action signs, the displayed convention means $\delta S_m=\tfrac12\int\sqrt{-g}\,T_{ab}\delta g^{ab}=-\tfrac12\int\sqrt{-g}\,T^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$. Fix the independent matter variables while varying the metric. Sign conventions must be matched to the action; copying a mostly-plus stress formula into this convention can reverse its energy density.

### Stress-energy tensor of a canonical scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

For signature $(+---)$ and $S_\phi=\int\sqrt{-g}[(\nabla\phi)^2/2-V(\phi)]$, the convention $T_{\mu\nu}=2(\delta S_\phi/\delta g^{\mu\nu})/\sqrt{-g}$ gives the displayed tensor. Varying the inverse metric yields the derivative product, while varying the volume element yields the term proportional to $g_{\mu\nu}$. A homogeneous field has energy density $\dot\phi^2/2+V$ and pressure $\dot\phi^2/2-V$.

### Stress-energy tensor improvement

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

Adding $\partial_\rho B^{\rho\mu\nu}$ with $B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}$ to a [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) preserves its divergence identically, since commuting partial derivatives annihilates the antisymmetric pair. The total [four-momentum](special-relativity.md#four-momentum) changes only by a spatial boundary term. Improvements can make local densities symmetric or [gauge-invariant](relativistic-quantum-field.md#gauge-invariance); equality of integrated charges requires the chosen [boundary conditions](differential-equation.md#boundary-condition). Equation-of-motion terms can also relate representatives [on shell](quantum-field-theory.md#on-shell).

#### Improved stress-energy tensor of a free massless scalar

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor improvement](#stress-energy-tensor-improvement)

For $d>1$, the displayed [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) is symmetric and conserved on the free massless [scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field) equation $\Box\phi=0$. Its improvement is identically divergence-free. Since $\Box\phi^2=2(\partial\phi)^2$ on shell, its trace vanishes. Contracting it with a [Conformal Killing vector field](#conformal-killing-vector-field) therefore produces a conserved conformal current. In $d=2$ the improvement coefficient is zero: the canonical tensor already has zero trace.

#### Stress-energy superpotential

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor improvement](#stress-energy-tensor-improvement)

A [stress-energy superpotential](#stress-energy-superpotential) is an antisymmetric tensor $B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}$ whose divergence supplies a [stress-energy tensor improvement](#stress-energy-tensor-improvement). In the [Maxwell field](electromagnetism.md#electromagnetic-field), the potential-dependent expression $B^{\rho\mu}{}_{\nu}=F^{\mu\rho}A_\nu$ produces the difference between canonical and gauge-invariant [stress-energy tensors](#stress-energy-tensor) [on shell](quantum-field-theory.md#on-shell). Antisymmetry ensures identically conserved improvement terms.

### Dust stress-energy tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

A pressureless fluid with proper number density $n_0$, rest mass $m$ and [four-velocity](special-relativity.md#four-velocity) $u$ has tensor $mn_0u^\mu u^\nu$. In its local rest frame there is energy density but no spatial stress. Particle-number conservation and the Lorentz force make its divergence the electromagnetic force density.

#### Null dust

↑ **Parent:** [Dust stress-energy tensor](#dust-stress-energy-tensor)

Null dust is matter with a [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) $T_{ab}=\varepsilon k_ak_b$ for a future-directed [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector) $k$. For every future-directed [timelike vector](#timelike-vector) $t$, the energy current is $-T^a{}_bt^b=\varepsilon(-k\cdot t)k^a$. Therefore its [dominant energy condition](#dominant-energy-condition) holds exactly when $\varepsilon\geq0$, allowing the zero current. Rescaling $k$ rescales $\varepsilon$ inversely by the square and leaves the [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) unchanged. Ingoing [Vaidya metric](#vaidya-metric) matter has $k=-\partial_r$, $k_a=-(dv)_a$, and $\varepsilon=\dot m/(4\pi r^2)$.

### Momentum density

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

[Momentum density](#momentum-density) is [momentum](classical-mechanics.md#momentum) per unit physical volume. In a local [orthonormal tetrad](#orthonormal-frame-in-spacetime) of the measuring observer it is $q^{\hat i}=T^{\hat0\hat i}$. A [perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity) moving at a small velocity relative to that observer has $q^{\hat i}=(\rho+P)v^{\hat i}$ to first order. For isotropic background [photons](quantum-mechanics.md#photon), $P=\rho/3$, so the factor is $4\rho/3$ rather than $\rho$. Integrating the [kinetic stress-energy tensor](statistical-physics.md#kinetic-stress-energy-tensor) gives $q^{\hat i}=\int f p^{\hat i}\,d^3p$.

### Anisotropic stress

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

In a local [orthonormal tetrad](#orthonormal-frame-in-spacetime), [anisotropic stress](#anisotropic-stress) is the trace-free spatial part of the [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor), conventionally denoted $\pi^{ij}$. Some [linear cosmological perturbation theory](linear-cosmological-perturbation-theory.md) conventions instead use $\Pi^{ij}=-\pi^{ij}$ and write $T^{ij}=P\delta^{ij}-\Pi^{ij}$. The sign must be specified before using a [photon Euler equation](cosmic-microwave-background-anisotropy.md#photon-euler-equation). The [photon quadrupole](cosmic-microwave-background-anisotropy.md#photon-quadrupole) determines the linear [photon](quantum-mechanics.md#photon) [anisotropic stress](#anisotropic-stress).

### Stress-energy tensor of a nonminimally coupled scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

For action $S=\int\sqrt{-g}[-\frac12(\nabla\Phi)^2-\xi R\Phi^2]d^4x$, [metric variation](#metric-variation) defines $\delta S=\frac12\int\sqrt{-g}T^{ab}\delta g_{ab}d^4x$ and gives the displayed tensor. The [contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity) and the scalar derivative commutator yield $\nabla^aT_{ab}=(\Box\Phi-2\xi R\Phi)\nabla_b\Phi$, so the [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) is conserved on the field equation.

### Stress-energy conservation

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

Local energy-momentum conservation is the covariant equation $\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0$. Its normal and spatial projections give energy and momentum evolution equations in a spacetime foliation.

#### Noether identity

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy conservation](#stress-energy-conservation)

A Noether identity is an off-shell differential relation among Euler-Lagrange expressions implied by a local gauge symmetry. Diffeomorphism invariance relates the divergence of the stress-energy tensor to the matter field equations.

##### Diffeomorphism identity for a metric action

↑ **Parent:** [Noether identity](#noether-identity)

For a diffeomorphism-invariant pure metric action, write $\delta S=-\int\sqrt{-g}E^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$. Substitute $\delta g_{ab}=\nabla_a\xi_b+\nabla_b\xi_a$ for an arbitrary compactly supported [vector field](calculus.md#vector-field) and integrate by parts. Symmetry of $E^{ab}$ gives $\nabla_aE^{ab}=0$ without using field equations. This [Noether identity](#noether-identity) concerns the gravitational variational tensor alone.

##### Diffeomorphism Noether identity for a scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Noether identity](#noether-identity)

Simultaneously varying a [metric tensor](#metric-tensor) and a [scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field) by the [Lie derivative](differential-form.md#lie-derivative-of-a-differential-form) along a compactly supported [vector field](calculus.md#vector-field) leaves a covariant matter action invariant. [Integration by parts](calculus.md#integration-by-parts) gives the displayed [Noether identity](#noether-identity). It implies [stress-energy conservation](#stress-energy-conservation) when the scalar equation holds without needing to impose the metric field equation.

### Perfect fluid in general relativity

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

A relativistic perfect fluid has energy density $\rho$, isotropic pressure $p$ and unit timelike four-velocity $u$. Projecting $\nabla_aT^{ab}=0$ along and orthogonally to $u$ gives its energy and Euler equations.

For a [perfect fluid](#perfect-fluid), the relativistic stress tensor is $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b+pg_{ab}$ in signature $(-,+,+,+)$ with $u^au_a=-1$.

#### Canonical scalar stress as a perfect fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

For a canonical scalar with timelike gradient, its [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) has perfect-fluid form. In a homogeneous background, $\rho=\dot\phi^2/2+V$ and $p=\dot\phi^2/2-V$. Potential domination gives negative pressure, while the scalar's physical rest-frame [sound speed](compressible-flow.md#speed-of-sound) is one. A constant negative background $w$ therefore does not force its perturbations to obey the adiabatic barotropic closure $\delta p=w\delta\rho$.

// Target: cosmology.bigb

##### Conformal density of a canonical scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Canonical scalar stress as a perfect fluid](#canonical-scalar-stress-as-a-perfect-fluid)

For $ds^2=a^2(d\tau^2-d\mathbf x^2)$ and a homogeneous canonical scalar, $\rho=\phi'^2/(2a^2)+V$ is the physical density, whereas $T_{00}=a^2\rho$. At $w=-1/3$, the kinetic and potential energies are $K=\rho/3$ and $V=2\rho/3$, so $\phi'^2=2a^2\rho/3=2T_{00}/3$. A single power of $\phi'$ cannot replace the square in this energy identity.

// Target: cosmology.bigb

#### Canonical vorticity of a relativistic fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

The canonical vorticity two-form is $\omega_{ab}=\nabla_a(\epsilon u_b)-\nabla_b(\epsilon u_a)$, with [specific enthalpy per particle](#specific-enthalpy-per-particle) $\epsilon$. Potential flow $\epsilon u_a=\nabla_a\chi$ has $\omega=0$ for a [torsion-free connection](fiber-bundle.md#torsion-free-connection). Contracting this identity with $u^a$ yields $\epsilon u^a\nabla_au_b=\nabla_b\epsilon-u_bu^a\nabla_a\epsilon$, the isentropic [relativistic Euler equation](#relativistic-euler-equation) in signature $(+---)$. This canonical condition does not mean the unweighted four-velocity one-form is closed.

#### Variational action for relativistic potential flow

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

In signature $(+---)$, the matter action $S=\int\sqrt{-g}[p(\epsilon)+n((\nabla\chi)^2-\epsilon^2)/(2\epsilon)]$ uses independent $n,\epsilon,\chi$. Variations of $n,\epsilon,\chi$ give $(\nabla\chi)^2=\epsilon^2$, $p'(\epsilon)=n$, and [particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid](#particle-number-conservation-in-a-relativistic-fluid) for $u_a=\nabla_a\chi/\epsilon$. Its [Hilbert stress-energy tensor](#hilbert-stress-energy-tensor) is $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b-pg_{ab}$. The action describes isentropic potential flow, with vanishing [canonical vorticity of a relativistic fluid](#canonical-vorticity-of-a-relativistic-fluid), rather than every vortical perfect-fluid solution.

#### Particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

The number current is $J^a=nu^a$, where $n$ is the rest-frame [number density](statistical-physics.md#number-density) and $u^a$ the unit [four-velocity](special-relativity.md#four-velocity). Its covariant continuity equation is $\nabla_aJ^a=0$, equivalently $u^a\nabla_an+n\nabla_au^a=0$. Integration over a spacetime volume and the [divergence theorem](calculus.md#divergence-theorem) equate the particle numbers crossing its boundary. This conservation law is separate from [stress-energy conservation](#stress-energy-conservation).

#### Specific enthalpy per particle

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

For [number density](statistical-physics.md#number-density) $n$, rest-frame [energy density](statistical-physics.md#energy-density) $\rho$ and [pressure](thermodynamics.md#pressure) $p$, the enthalpy per particle is $\epsilon=(\rho+p)/n$. In an isentropic [barotropic fluid](fluid-mechanics.md#barotropic-fluid), $d\rho=\epsilon\,dn$ and $dp=n\,d\epsilon$. It differs from enthalpy per unit mass by the particle mass when that mass is fixed. Using $\epsilon$ as thermodynamic variable gives $n=p'(\epsilon)$ and $\rho=\epsilon p'(\epsilon)-p(\epsilon)$.

##### Barotropic relativistic sound speed

↑ **Parent:** [Specific enthalpy per particle](#specific-enthalpy-per-particle)

In units $c=1$, an isentropic [perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity) has sound speed $c_s^2=dp/d\rho$. Writing $n=p'(\epsilon)$ gives $d\rho=\epsilon\,dn$ and $c_s^2=n/(\epsilon p''(\epsilon))$. Linearized [particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid](#particle-number-conservation-in-a-relativistic-fluid) and the [relativistic Euler equation](#relativistic-euler-equation) then yield acoustic waves with this speed. Positive $c_s^2$ is needed for ordinary acoustic stability; a causal fluid model additionally requires $c_s^2\le1$.

#### Perfect-fluid alignment in a static spacetime

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

For [metric signature](topology.md#metric-signature) $(+---)$, a [perfect fluid](#perfect-fluid) has $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b-pg_{ab}$. The [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) and [static Killing field is a Ricci eigenvector](#static-killing-field-is-a-ricci-eigenvector) imply $(\rho+p)(k\cdot u)u_{[a}k_{b]}=0$. Two nonzero timelike vectors cannot be orthogonal, so $k\cdot u\ne0$. If $\rho+p\ne0$, the [four-velocity](special-relativity.md#four-velocity) is parallel to the static [Killing vector](#killing-vector-field). Choosing both future-directed gives the displayed normalization. For $\rho+p=0$, the [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) is proportional to the [metric tensor](#metric-tensor) and does not determine a preferred fluid velocity; the strict enthalpy hypothesis is therefore material.

#### Stiff fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

A [perfect fluid](#perfect-fluid) with [equation of state](thermodynamics.md#equation-of-state) $P=\rho$ is called a stiff fluid. For positive [energy density](statistical-physics.md#energy-density), its constant equation-of-state parameter is $w=1$. The [cosmological continuity equation](cosmology.md#cosmological-continuity-equation) gives $\rho\propto a^{-6}$. In a flat universe dominated by this fluid, the [Friedmann equation](cosmology.md#friedmann-equations) then gives $a(t)\propto(t-t_B)^{1/3}$. This statement assumes a nonzero fluid density and negligible other components.

##### Homogeneous free scalar as a stiff fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Stiff fluid](#stiff-fluid)

For a spatially homogeneous [minimally coupled scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#minimally-coupled-scalar-field) with zero [scalar potential](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-potential), the [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) has $\rho=P=\dot\phi^2/2$. Its field equation is $\ddot\phi+3H\dot\phi=0$, so $a^3\dot\phi$ is constant and its [energy density](statistical-physics.md#energy-density) decreases as $a^{-6}$. Thus its homogeneous stress is precisely that of a [stiff fluid](#stiff-fluid). Spatial gradients would introduce directional stresses, so this equivalence requires homogeneity.

###### Cosmological contraction with a free homogeneous scalar

↑ **Parent:** [Homogeneous free scalar as a stiff fluid](#homogeneous-free-scalar-as-a-stiff-fluid)

In units $8\pi G=1$, a free homogeneous scalar has $\rho=P=\dot\phi_0^2/2$ and therefore a stiff equation of state. Its equation gives $a^2\phi_0'=\text{constant}$, while the [Friedmann equation](cosmology.md#friedmann-equations) gives $3\mathcal H^2=\phi_0'^2/2$. The displayed contracting branch satisfies both equations for negative [conformal time](cosmology.md#conformal-time). Constant normalizations and an additive scalar shift can be restored.

###### Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction

↑ **Parent:** [Cosmological contraction with a free homogeneous scalar](#cosmological-contraction-with-a-free-homogeneous-scalar)

In [conformal Newtonian gauge](linear-cosmological-perturbation-theory.md#newtonian-gauge), a free scalar has no linear anisotropic stress. Eliminating its perturbation with the momentum constraint gives $\Phi_k''+3\Phi_k'/\tau+k^2\Phi_k=0$. Substitution of $\Phi_k=(-\tau)^{-1}y(-k\tau)$ gives the order-one [Bessel equation](analysis.md#bessel-differential-equation). The displayed [Hankel function](analysis.md#hankel-function) has positive incoming frequency $e^{-ik\tau}$ at early times.

###### Vacuum-normalized Newtonian spectrum in stiff contraction

↑ **Parent:** [Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction](#newtonian-potential-hankel-modes-in-stiff-contraction)

The momentum constraint relates the incoming scalar mode $a^{-1}\sqrt{\hbar/(2k)}e^{-ik\tau}$ to the [Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction](#newtonian-potential-hankel-modes-in-stiff-contraction). Large-argument Hankel asymptotics give $|N_k|^2=3\pi\hbar/(32k^2)$. The small-argument behavior $H_1^{(1)}(z)\sim-2i/(\pi z)$ then gives the displayed spectrum. Its dimensional power is $k^{-4}$ and its power per logarithmic interval is proportional to $k^{-1}$. These are Newtonian-potential spectra; a curvature-perturbation spectrum need not have the same exponent.

#### Relativistic perfect-fluid energy equation

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

The timelike projection of stress-energy conservation is $u^a\nabla_a\rho+(\rho+p)\nabla_au^a=0$. It is the local adiabatic first law for a perfect fluid.

#### Relativistic Euler equation

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)

The spatial projection of stress-energy conservation is $(\rho+p)u^b\nabla_bu^a=-(g^{ab}+u^au^b)\nabla_bp$.

##### Weak-field slow-motion equations for a relativistic fluid

↑ **Parent:** [Relativistic Euler equation](#relativistic-euler-equation)

For $ds^2=(1+2\phi)dt^2-d\mathbf x^2$ with static weak $\phi$, normalization gives $u^0=1-\phi+O(\phi^2,v^2)$ and $\Gamma^i{}_{00}=\partial_i\phi$. Around a homogeneous state, the first-order equations are $\partial_t\delta n+n_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf v=0$, $\partial_t\delta\rho+(\rho_0+p_0)\nabla\cdot\mathbf v=0$, and $(\rho_0+p_0)\partial_t\mathbf v=-\nabla\delta p-(\rho_0+p_0)\nabla\phi$. The inertial density is enthalpy density even though the flow is slow. Only a further nonrelativistic equation of state, $p\ll\rho\simeq mn$, replaces it by mass density.

##### Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector

↑ **Parent:** [Relativistic Euler equation](#relativistic-euler-equation)

For [metric signature](topology.md#metric-signature) $(+---)$, a stationary [perfect fluid](#perfect-fluid) follows a timelike [Killing vector field](#killing-vector-field) $K$ with [four-velocity](special-relativity.md#four-velocity) $U=K/N$. Its [Killing-flow four-acceleration](#killing-flow-four-acceleration) is $U^b\nabla_bU_a=-\nabla_a\log N$. The [relativistic Euler equation](#relativistic-euler-equation) and stationarity of the [pressure](thermodynamics.md#pressure) give the displayed [hydrostatic equilibrium](statistical-physics.md#hydrostatic-equilibrium) equation. This uses stationarity along $K$ and does not require hypersurface orthogonality.

###### Stationary radiation density

↑ **Parent:** [Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector](#hydrostatic-equilibrium-along-a-killing-vector)

The [equation of state](thermodynamics.md#equation-of-state) of isotropic [blackbody radiation](statistical-physics.md#black-body-radiation) is $p=\rho/3$. The [barotropic hydrostatic first integral](#barotropic-hydrostatic-first-integral) gives $\rho N^4=C$. The [Stefan–Boltzmann law](thermodynamics.md#stefan-boltzmann-law) $\rho\propto T^4$ then yields the [Tolman–Ehrenfest relation](thermodynamics.md#tolman-ehrenfest-relation) $TN=\mathrm{constant}$. Here $N=\sqrt{|K^aK_a|}$ for the stationary timelike [Killing vector field](#killing-vector-field); its normalization only changes the constants.

###### Radiation fluid has no regular free surface

↑ **Parent:** [Stationary radiation density](#stationary-radiation-density)

A connected nonvacuum [perfect fluid](#perfect-fluid) with [equation of state](thermodynamics.md#equation-of-state) $p=\rho/3$ obeys the [stationary radiation density](#stationary-radiation-density) law $\rho=C/N^4$ with $C>0$. On a compact region of a regular [spacetime](special-relativity.md#spacetime), a smooth [Killing vector field](#killing-vector-field) has bounded norm. Consequently $\rho$ cannot tend to zero at a finite regular [free surface](fluid-mechanics.md#free-surface). A vanishing timelike norm instead makes the density diverge. A radiation-filled cavity can have a material wall with nonzero boundary [pressure](thermodynamics.md#pressure); this is a different boundary condition.

###### Barotropic hydrostatic first integral

↑ **Parent:** [Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector](#hydrostatic-equilibrium-along-a-killing-vector)

For a [barotropic fluid](fluid-mechanics.md#barotropic-fluid) in [hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector](#hydrostatic-equilibrium-along-a-killing-vector), assume $\rho+p>0$. Integrating $dp/(\rho+p)=-d\log N$ gives the displayed [first integral](differential-equation.md#first-integral), constant on each connected fluid region. For the [equation of state](thermodynamics.md#equation-of-state) $p=w\rho$ with constant $w>0$, it gives $\rho N^{(1+w)/w}=C_1$.

<h4 id="tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation">Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation</h4>

↑ **Parent:** [Perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff_equation)

The Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation governs a static, spherically symmetric [perfect fluid in general relativity](#perfect-fluid-in-general-relativity). In geometrized units, if $m(r)$ is the mass inside areal radius $r$, then

$$
\frac{dm}{dr}=4\pi r^2\rho,
\qquad
\frac{dp}{dr}=-\frac{(\rho+p)(m+4\pi r^3p)}{r(r-2m)}.
$$

##### Stellar compactness

↑ **Parent:** [Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation](#tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation)

The ratio of enclosed gravitational mass to areal radius in units $G=c=1$. In a spherical star the local value is $m(r)/r$, and the surface value is $M/R$. Bounds on this ratio constrain relativistic stellar models; it is unrelated to topological compactness.

##### Regular central expansion of the TOV equations

↑ **Parent:** [Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation](#tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation)

For a smooth centre with density $\rho_c$ and pressure $p_c$, the [Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation](#tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation) gives $m(r)=4\pi\rho_cr^3/3+O(r^5)$ and $p(r)=p_c-2\pi(\rho_c+p_c)(\rho_c/3+p_c)r^2+O(r^4)$. A fixed [barotropic stellar equation of state](thermodynamics.md#barotropic-stellar-equation-of-state) leaves one physical central parameter; the additive lapse constant fixes time normalization.

##### Interior Schwarzschild metric

↑ **Parent:** [Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation](#tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Schwarzschild_metric)

The interior Schwarzschild metric is the exact static solution for a spherical fluid of constant density. If the star has mass $M$ and radius $R$, its pressure is

$$
p(r)=\rho
\frac{\sqrt{1-2Mr^2/R^3}-\sqrt{1-2M/R}}
{3\sqrt{1-2M/R}-\sqrt{1-2Mr^2/R^3}}.
$$

###### Central pressure of a constant-density relativistic star

↑ **Parent:** [Interior Schwarzschild metric](#interior-schwarzschild-metric)

The [Interior Schwarzschild metric](#interior-schwarzschild-metric) has central pressure

$$
p(0)=\rho
\frac{1-\sqrt{1-2M/R}}
{3\sqrt{1-2M/R}-1}.
$$

It diverges when $M/R=4/9$, the limiting compactness in [Buchdahl's theorem](#buchdahl-s-theorem).

###### Dominant-energy compactness bound for an incompressible star

↑ **Parent:** [Central pressure of a constant-density relativistic star](#central-pressure-of-a-constant-density-relativistic-star)

The [dominant energy condition](#dominant-energy-condition) for the [Interior Schwarzschild metric](#interior-schwarzschild-metric) reduces to its largest pressure satisfying $p_c\leq\rho$. Substitution of the exact central pressure gives $\sqrt{1-2M/R}\geq1/2$, hence $M/R\leq3/8$. Equality has finite central pressure and is allowed by the non-strict condition.

<h5 id="buchdahl-s-theorem">Buchdahl's theorem</h5>

↑ **Parent:** [Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation](#tolman-oppenheimer-volkoff-equation)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buchdahl's_theorem)

Buchdahl's theorem gives $2M/R\leq8/9$ for a static spherical perfect-fluid star whose density is nonincreasing outwards, subject to its regularity assumptions. Equality would require divergent central pressure, so every regular such star satisfies $M/R<4/9$.

###### High-density core bound from TOV compactness

↑ **Parent:** [Buchdahl's theorem](#buchdahl-s-theorem)

Let $\rho_0>0$ be a density threshold and let $a,\mu$ be the radius and enclosed mass where a monotonically decreasing stellar density reaches that threshold. Then $\mu\geq4\pi\rho_0a^3/3$. A local compactness bound $\mu/a<4/9$ implies $a<(3\pi\rho_0)^{-1/2}$ and $\mu<4/(9\sqrt{3\pi\rho_0})$, independently of the [equation of state](thermodynamics.md#equation-of-state) above the threshold. A total-mass bound also requires suitable control of the known low-density envelope.

### Null energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Null_energy_condition)

The null energy condition requires $T_{\mu\nu}k^\mu k^\nu\geq0$ for every null vector $k$. Through the Einstein field equations this is equivalent to $R_{\mu\nu}k^\mu k^\nu\geq0$ when the cosmological term is absent.

#### Perfect-fluid null energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Null energy condition](#null-energy-condition)

For $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b+pg_{ab}$ and nonzero null $k$, contraction gives $T_{ab}k^ak^b=(\rho+p)(u\cdot k)^2$. A timelike fluid velocity has nonzero scalar product with every nonzero null vector, so the [null energy condition](#null-energy-condition) is exactly the displayed inequality. Individual nonnegativity of pressure or density is unnecessary for null focusing. A [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant) term has zero null contraction.

#### Null energy condition for a canonical scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Null energy condition](#null-energy-condition)

The classical [stress-energy tensor of a canonical scalar field](#stress-energy-tensor-of-a-canonical-scalar-field) has a gradient-product term and a metric-proportional term. Contracting with a real [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector) removes the latter, leaving the displayed square for a real field. This proves the [null energy condition](#null-energy-condition) for a minimally coupled canonical scalar, including the massless case. It is not a statement about arbitrary curvature couplings, wrong-sign kinetic terms or a renormalized quantum [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor).

#### Null energy condition for a planar warped spacetime

↑ **Parent:** [Null energy condition](#null-energy-condition)

In a [planar warped spacetime](#planar-warped-spacetime), contraction of the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor) with a [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector) in an [orthonormal frame](#orthonormal-frame-in-spacetime) gives $R_{ab}k^ak^b=-2(\log A)''(k^3)^2$. The [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) identify this with $8\pi T_{ab}k^ak^b$; metric terms, including a cosmological constant, vanish in a null contraction. Thus the [null energy condition](#null-energy-condition) is equivalent to concavity of $\log A$ in the proper transverse coordinate $z$. Null directions confined to a planar slice saturate the condition.

#### Null convergence condition

↑ **Parent:** [Null energy condition](#null-energy-condition)

The [null convergence condition](#null-convergence-condition) requires $R_{ab}k^ak^b\geq0$ for every [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector). Through the [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations), it follows from the [null energy condition](#null-energy-condition), since all terms proportional to the [metric tensor](#metric-tensor) vanish on a [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector).

##### Cosmological constant preserves null convergence

↑ **Parent:** [Null convergence condition](#null-convergence-condition)

Contract the [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) with a [null vector](special-relativity.md#null-vector). Both the trace term and the [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant) term multiply $g_{ab}\ell^a\ell^b=0$ and disappear. The [null energy condition](#null-energy-condition) therefore still implies the [null convergence condition](#null-convergence-condition), independently of the sign of the [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant). A [black-hole area theorem](#hawking-s-area-theorem) still needs the appropriate global horizon assumptions as well as this local focusing condition.

#### Averaged null energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Null energy condition](#null-energy-condition)

The averaged null energy condition requires

$$
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}T_{ab}k^ak^b\,d\lambda\geq0
$$

along every complete affinely parametrized null geodesic. It is weaker than the pointwise null energy condition.

##### Averaged null energy condition for a nonminimally coupled scalar

↑ **Parent:** [Averaged null energy condition](#averaged-null-energy-condition)

For the curvature-coupled scalar equation considered here, contraction with an affine null tangent and integration by parts gives, for $\xi<0$,

$$
\int T_{ab}k^ak^b\,d\lambda
=\int\frac{1+\xi(4\xi-1)\Phi^2}{(1-\xi\Phi^2)^2}(\Phi')^2\,d\lambda\geq0
$$

when $\Phi\Phi'/(1-\xi\Phi^2)$ vanishes at the endpoints.

### Conserved Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy ansatz

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

For $\nabla_\alpha\nabla^\alpha\phi=m^2\phi$, consider

$$
T_{\mu\nu}=\frac12\left[
\nabla_\mu\phi\nabla_\nu\phi
+g_{\mu\nu}\left(A\nabla_\rho\phi\nabla^\rho\phi+B\phi^2\right)
\right].
$$

Covariant conservation for all solutions holds exactly when

$$
A=-\frac12,
\qquad
B=-\frac{m^2}{2}.
$$

### Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)

For a real scalar satisfying $\nabla_a\nabla^a\psi-\mu^2\psi=0$,

$$
T_{ab}=\nabla_a\psi\nabla_b\psi-\frac12g_{ab}\left(\nabla_c\psi\nabla^c\psi+\mu^2\psi^2\right)
$$

is symmetric and covariantly conserved.

### Weak energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weak_energy_condition)

The weak energy condition requires

$$
T_{\mu\nu}X^\mu X^\nu\geq0
$$

for every timelike vector $X$, so every observer measures nonnegative energy density.

#### Weak energy condition for a quadratic scalar stress-energy ansatz

↑ **Parent:** [Weak energy condition](#weak-energy-condition)

For the general scalar ansatz in [Conserved Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy ansatz](#conserved-klein-gordon-scalar-stress-energy-ansatz), work in a unit timelike frame. Then

$$
2T_{00}=(1+A)(\partial_0\phi)^2
-A|\nabla_{\rm s}\phi|^2-B\phi^2.
$$

The weak energy condition for arbitrary local field data is therefore equivalent to

$$
-1\leq A\leq0,
\qquad B\leq0.
$$

### Strong energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strong_energy_condition)

The strong energy condition requires $(T_{ab}-Tg_{ab}/2)u^au^b\geq0$ for every timelike vector $u$ in four spacetime dimensions. Through the [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations), it implies the timelike convergence condition $R_{ab}u^au^b\geq0$ when the cosmological constant vanishes.

### Dominant energy condition

↑ **Parent:** [Stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominant_energy_condition)

The dominant energy condition requires the energy flux measured by every future-directed timelike observer to be future-directed and causal. Equivalently, $T_{ab}u^av^b\geq0$ for every pair of future-directed causal vectors $u$ and $v$ under the $(-,+,+,+)$ convention.

#### Dominant energy condition for a kinetic scalar field

↑ **Parent:** [Dominant energy condition](#dominant-energy-condition)

For a [scalar field](quantum-field-theory.md#scalar-field) with $X=-q^2/2$, Lagrangian $F(X)$ and [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) $T_{ab}=F\prime q_aq_b+Fg_{ab}$, the inequalities $A=F\prime\ge0$ and $Y=XF\prime-F\ge0$ imply the [dominant energy condition](#dominant-energy-condition) for any gradient type. In a local [Lorentz frame](#lorentz-frame), $\rho=A(q_0^2+\sum_iq_i^2)/2+Y\ge0$ and $\rho^2-\sum_iT_{0i}^2=A^2X^2+A(q_0^2+\sum_iq_i^2)Y+Y^2\ge0$.

## Einstein field equations

↑ **Parent:** [General relativity](general-relativity.md)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein_field_equations)

With signature $(-,+,+,+)$ and vanishing cosmological constant, the Einstein field equations are

$$
R_{\mu\nu}-\frac12 g_{\mu\nu}R=\kappa T_{\mu\nu}.
$$

In four spacetime dimensions, contraction with $g^{\mu\nu}$ gives the useful trace relation $R=-\kappa T$.

### Einstein equation in the sequential-derivative curvature convention

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

With signature $(+---)$, let $R^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_d\Gamma^a{}_{bc}-\partial_c\Gamma^a{}_{bd}-\Gamma^a{}_{pc}\Gamma^p{}_{bd}+\Gamma^a{}_{pd}\Gamma^p{}_{bc}$ and $R_{ab}=R^c{}_{acb}$. This curvature is the negative of the convention in which $[\nabla_c,\nabla_d]X^a=R^a{}_{bcd}X^b$. The physical [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) are $G_{ab}+\Lambda g_{ab}=-8\pi G T_{ab}/c^4$. In a static weak field $R_{00}=-\nabla^2\Phi/c^2$; the trace-reversed equation then reproduces the [Poisson equation](partial-differential-equation.md#poisson-equation) $\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G\rho_{\rm mass}$. Metric variation of $\int\sqrt{-g}(R-2\Lambda)/(2\kappa)+S_m$ gives the same sign when $\delta S_m=\int\sqrt{-g}T_{ab}\delta g^{ab}/2$.

### Newtonian normalization of the Einstein field equations

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

Assume a metric theory in four dimensions, a [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection), covariant [stress-energy conservation](#stress-energy-conservation) and field equations linear in second metric derivatives. The [contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity) selects $G_{ab}=R_{ab}-g_{ab}R/2$. Its trace-reversed equation is $R_{ab}=\kappa(T_{ab}-g_{ab}T/2)+\Lambda g_{ab}$. In the convention $R_{00}\simeq-\nabla^2\varphi$ and signature $(+---)$, nonrelativistic matter gives $R_{00}\simeq\kappa\rho/2$. The [Poisson equation](partial-differential-equation.md#poisson-equation) $\nabla^2\varphi=4\pi G\rho$ fixes $\kappa=-8\pi G$. Reversing the curvature convention reverses this displayed coupling sign without changing the physical theory. The [strong equivalence principle](#strong-equivalence-principle) alone does not establish uniqueness without the stated additional assumptions.

### Einstein-equation coupling under reversed curvature

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

With the metric and [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor) fixed and zero [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant), changing the sign convention for the [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) changes the signs of the [Ricci tensor](#ricci-tensor), [scalar curvature](second-fundamental-form.md#scalar-curvature) and [Einstein tensor](#einstein-tensor). The same physical field equation is therefore written with the opposite coupling sign. For signature $(+---)$, the convention $R^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_d\Gamma^a_{bc}-\partial_c\Gamma^a_{bd}-\Gamma^a_{ec}\Gamma^e_{bd}+\Gamma^a_{ed}\Gamma^e_{bc}$ and $R_{ab}=R^c{}_{acb}$ gives $R_{00}\simeq-\Delta\Phi$ in a static [Newtonian limit](#newtonian-limit). The trace-reversed equation and $\Delta\Phi=4\pi G\rho$ then require $G_{ab}=-8\pi GT_{ab}$.

### Planar Einstein metric with cubic radial function

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

This metric has $R_{ab}=-3g_{ab}$ and [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant) $\Lambda=-3$ in unit curvature-radius normalization. Its regular static coframe is real when $F>0$ and $z\ne0$. Setting $q=\alpha z^3$, its six independent [curvature 2-form](connection-1-form.md#curvature-2-form) coefficients are $-(1-q)$ in the $03$ and $12$ planes and $-(1+q/2)$ in the other four planes. Their Ricci contractions cancel $q$. The [Weyl tensor](#weyl-tensor) squared invariant is $12\alpha^2z^6$, so nonzero $\alpha$ does not give constant sectional curvature despite the Einstein equation.

<h3 id="lovelock-s-theorem">Lovelock's theorem</h3>

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

In four dimensions, a local natural symmetric divergence-free metric tensor involving the metric and its derivatives of at most second order has the Lovelock form $aG_{ab}+bg_{ab}$ with constant coefficients. Generic nonlinear metric [f(R) gravity](#f-r-gravity) instead has fourth-order terms and does not meet that derivative-order hypothesis. For affine $f$, the metric field equation is precisely of Lovelock form.

### Vacuum Einstein equations

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)

In a region with vanishing [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor), the [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations) reduce to $R_{\mu\nu}=0$ when the [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant) vanishes.

#### Vacuum spacetime

↑ **Parent:** [Vacuum Einstein equations](#vacuum-einstein-equations)

A [vacuum spacetime](#vacuum-spacetime) has vanishing matter [stress-energy tensor](#stress-energy-tensor). In $D>2$ dimensions, tracing the [Vacuum Einstein equations](#vacuum-einstein-equations) gives $R=2D\Lambda/(D-2)$ and hence $R_{ab}=2\Lambda g_{ab}/(D-2)$; in four dimensions this reduces to $R_{ab}=\Lambda g_{ab}$. With zero [cosmological constant](cosmology.md#cosmological-constant) it is [Ricci flat](differential-geometry.md#ricci-flat-manifold), although its full [Riemann curvature tensor](#riemann-curvature-tensor) can be nonzero: a vacuum [Kerr black hole](#kerr-black-hole) and vacuum [gravitational waves](#gravitational-wave) illustrate that distinction.

### Einstein-Hilbert action

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein-Hilbert_action)

The Einstein-Hilbert action is the integral of the [Ricci scalar](#ricci-scalar) against the metric volume form. Varying it with respect to the metric gives the vacuum [Einstein field equations](#einstein-field-equations), up to a boundary term.

#### Einstein-Cartan theory

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Hilbert action](#einstein-hilbert-action)  
ⓦ [Wiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein-Cartan_theory)

An independent [vierbein](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime) and metric-compatible [spin connection](connection-1-form.md#spin-connection) allow [spin](quantum-mechanics.md#spin) density to source a [torsion tensor](fiber-bundle.md#torsion-tensor). The connection equation from the [Einstein-Hilbert action](#einstein-hilbert-action) is algebraic in torsion. In the absence of connection-dependent matter it fixes the [Levi-Civita connection](#levi-civita-connection). For a minimally coupled [Dirac field](relativistic-quantum-field.md#dirac-field), eliminating the connection generates a local four-fermion interaction. Thus its equivalent torsion-free second-order [action](classical-mechanics.md#action) includes this contact term; simply dropping it defines a different matter theory.

<h5 id="1-5-order-formalism">1.5-order formalism</h5>

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Cartan theory](#einstein-cartan-theory)

Solve the algebraic connection equation for $\widehat\omega(e,\chi)$, but perform the remaining variations as though the connection were independent. The chain-rule contribution $(\delta S/\delta\omega)\delta\widehat\omega$ vanishes on that solution. The resulting equations are those of the connection-eliminated second-order [action](classical-mechanics.md#action). This is especially useful for local [supersymmetry](supersymmetry.md) calculations, where the gravitino-induced contorsion is quadratic in [fermions](quantum-mechanics.md#fermion) and the omitted induced connection variation would otherwise be lengthy.

##### Algebraic torsion of a Dirac field

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Cartan theory](#einstein-cartan-theory)

Use $K_{abc}=e_a{}^\mu(\omega-\omega(e))_{\mu bc}$ and $T_{abc}=K_{bac}-K_{cab}$. For the symmetrized Dirac [action](classical-mechanics.md#action) $-e\bar\chi\gamma^\mu\overleftrightarrow\nabla_\mu\chi/2-em\bar\chi\chi$, varying the [spin connection](connection-1-form.md#spin-connection) gives $-e\bar\chi\gamma^{\mu ab}\chi\,\delta\omega_{\mu ab}/4$. Only totally antisymmetric contorsion is sourced. Its [action](classical-mechanics.md#action) is $-eK^2/(2\kappa^2)-eK_{abc}\bar\chi\gamma^{abc}\chi/4$, hence the stated solution. Substitution generates $e\kappa^2(\bar\chi\gamma^{abc}\chi)(\bar\chi\gamma_{abc}\chi)/32$. The torsion is an [auxiliary field](supersymmetry.md#auxiliary-field), not an independent propagating degree of freedom.

#### First-order three-dimensional gravity

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Hilbert action](#einstein-hilbert-action)

For a nondegenerate [orthonormal coframe in spacetime](#orthonormal-coframe-in-spacetime) $E^a$ and independent antisymmetric Lorentz [connection 1-form](connection-1-form.md), the action $\int\epsilon_{abc}(R^{ab}\wedge E^c+\lambda E^a\wedge E^b\wedge E^c)$ has independent coframe and connection variations. Since $\delta R^{ab}=D\delta\omega^{ab}$, integration by parts yields a connection equation $\epsilon_{abc}T^c=0$. The three variations of the cubic coframe term give the coframe equation $\epsilon_{abc}(R^{ab}+3\lambda E^a\wedge E^b)=0$. In three dimensions these are exactly the equations displayed above. The solution has [constant sectional curvature](#constant-sectional-curvature) $K=-3\lambda$. Omitting the factor of three changes the cosmological normalization.

#### Einstein-Proca theory

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Hilbert action](#einstein-hilbert-action)

Einstein-Proca theory couples [general relativity](general-relativity.md) to a massive vector field governed by the [Proca action](electromagnetism.md#proca-action). Its matter stress-energy tensor is

$$
T_{ab}=F_{ac}F_b{}^c-\frac14g_{ab}F_{cd}F^{cd}
+m^2\left(A_aA_b-\frac12g_{ab}A_cA^c\right).
$$

The [contracted Bianchi identity](#contracted-bianchi-identity) makes the Einstein equation compatible with the covariant [Proca equation](electromagnetism.md#proca-equation).

##### Proca stress-energy tensor

↑ **Parent:** [Einstein-Proca theory](#einstein-proca-theory)

The Proca stress-energy tensor is the metric variation of the matter part of the [Proca action](electromagnetism.md#proca-action).
